I actually finally caved and bought these orthopedic inserts last month—total game changer, by the way. I got them at that pharmacy down on 4th street, the one with the really grumpy pharmacist who always looks like he's judging whatever you buy? I went in there to get the inserts and a pack of peppermint gum, and he just glared at me the whole time I was checking out... \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, blaming myself. Yeah, so I had to say no to mentoring Miss Davis because I just don't have the capacity for it right now. And when I do things like that, or when I'm just dragging through my work, I just tell myself I’m letting my family down, and my school down. It's just this constant feeling that I'm dropping the ball.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, I feel guilty and exhausted, but I don't want to hurt myself. I just want to feel better, you know? Like, I'd really love to just take a vacation somewhere and rest. My friend Sarah just got back from this trip to Sedona, and she was telling me all about these vortexes and how the energy there is supposed to be so healing. She brought back this little rose quartz crystal for me. I put it on my desk next to my pen cup—the one shaped like a little school bus that one of my students got me for Teacher Appreciation Week last year. It’s actually really cute, but the wheels keep falling off. I’ve tried supergluing them back on like three times, but I always manage to glue my fingers together instead. The last time I did it, I had to use nail polish remover to get my fingers unstuck, and it completely ruined my manicure. I usually just do a clear coat, but I had actually tried this really pretty pale pink color for my cousin's wedding... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, right, hurting myself. No, definitely not. I don't have any thoughts like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these feelings of exhaustion and low mood have been present nearly every day, and last throughout most of the day, even when you're not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess so? I'm not really sure if it's every single day, or if it lasts all day long. It's hard to keep track of time lately. Speaking of time, the clock in my classroom has been broken for like three weeks now. The second hand just twitches in place, right on the twelve. It makes this little *tick-tick-tick* sound that drives me absolutely up the wall when the kids are doing silent reading. I put in a maintenance request with Gary, our custodian, but he's been super busy because someone clogged the boys' bathroom on the second floor with paper towels. Gary's great, though. He always brings his golden retriever, Buster, to the school on weekends when he's doing deep cleaning. Buster is so fluffy, he looks like a giant toasted marshmallow. I tried to bring him some dog treats once, but they got squished in my bag under a stack of math worksheets... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, about the exhaustion being there every day. Yeah, I don't know if it's constantly there or not. I haven't really paid that much attention to exactly when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if most days you feel this low mood and lack of energy, even if some days are a little better than others?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... yeah, most days. Like, I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, you know? I have to. Especially with my third graders, they pick up on everything. Yesterday, little Tommy noticed I wasn't wearing my usual blue cardigan and asked if I was sick. He's so observant. His mom actually volunteers in the library on Tuesdays—she makes these incredible snickerdoodle cookies. She brought a batch in last week in this vintage Tupperware container. It was one of those harvest gold ones from the 70s? My grandmother used to have a whole set of those. She kept her sewing supplies in the big one, and I remember I’d always get so disappointed when I’d open it expecting cookies and just find spools of thread and needles... \n\nUm, sorry, what were we saying? Oh, right. Most days. Yeah, even when I'm smiling for the kids, underneath it all there’s just this... this gray, flat feeling. It just follows me home. And then even the things I usually like, like my garden or my watercolors, they just seem so far away. Like I just don't have the energy to even look at them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me when you first started noticing these feelings of exhaustion and low mood—was it more than two weeks ago, and have they been different from how you usually feel?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, no, it's been way longer than that. It's been going on for about two years and two months now. I know exactly when it started because it was right around the time my husband stopped working outside the home. Our whole household routine just completely flipped upside down. He started doing this freelance graphic design stuff from the guest room, which is great, but he completely took over the space. He bought this massive ergonomic chair that looks like something out of a spaceship, and it barely fits through the door. And he drinks so much coffee now, he bought one of those fancy espresso machines that grinds the beans right there. It sounds like a jet engine taking off every morning at 7 AM. I used to like the smell of coffee, but now it just reminds me of how messy the kitchen counter gets because he never wipes up the coffee grounds. I tried buying him one of those little countertop vacuums—it looked like a ladybug, it was so cute—but he said it didn't have enough suction. I actually ended up taking the ladybug vacuum to my classroom to clean up glitter after art projects. Glitter is just impossible, isn't it? It gets everywhere. I found a piece of green glitter on my forehead at the grocery store three days after we made St. Patrick's Day leprechaun traps... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, the timeline. Yeah, it's been about two years and two months since this whole exhausted, gray feeling started. Definitely way more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any big changes with that. I mean, I still eat my lunch at the same time every day. We have this really strict schedule at school, so lunch is exactly at 11:45. It's so early, right? But if you don't eat then, you just don't get a break until the kids go to P.E. at 1:30. The cafeteria ladies are usually pretty nice, though. Brenda, she's the one who always wears those holiday-themed aprons—she had one with little dancing turkeys on it last November—she always makes sure to give me an extra scoop of corn if they have it. She says I look like I need the energy. I actually bumped into Brenda at the farmer's market last weekend. She was buying these massive heirloom tomatoes. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels kept taking one bite out of them and leaving them on the fence. It's so frustrating! I bought this special netting to put over the plants, but it just got tangled in the rose bushes... \n\nWait, sorry, you were asking about my appetite? Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed eating any more or less than usual. Just the normal amount, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't seem to sit still and feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? No, I don't think so. I haven't really felt restless either. I mean, I have to be pretty quick on my feet with the kids. Just yesterday, one of my students, Sarah—she's the sweetest thing, always wears these little mismatched socks with cartoon characters on them—she accidentally knocked over the entire bin of math manipulatives. You know, those little plastic cubes that snap together? They went everywhere. It sounded like a hailstorm hitting the linoleum floor. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he just gave me this look when he walked by. He's been working at the school for like thirty years, I swear he knows every scuff mark in that building. He actually helped me fix my car once when it wouldn't start in the parking lot. It turned out to be the battery, which was weird because I just got it replaced at this place down on 4th Street. The guy there had this huge parrot in the waiting room that kept whistling the theme song to The Andy Griffith Show... \n\nUm, anyway, sorry. No, I don't feel like I'm moving slow or anything like that. I just feel tired, you know? But not restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't think so. I can still focus just fine. I mean, I have to be pretty sharp to keep up with all the grading and lesson plans. Just the other day I was going through the kids' science projects—we're doing the solar system right now—and I had to figure out how to grade this one project where a kid used actual fruit for the planets. He used a whole watermelon for Jupiter. It was incredibly creative, but it started leaking all over the display table in the back of the room. We had to put paper towels under it. The school buys those really cheap, scratchy brown paper towels, you know? The ones that don't actually absorb any water, they just kind of push the puddle around the table. \n\nI usually just buy my own rolls of Bounty to keep in the classroom closet. I get them in bulk at Costco. I love Costco, but the parking lot on a Sunday is just an absolute nightmare. Last time I went, someone dinged my car door with their cart and didn't even leave a note. People can be so inconsiderate, right? I had to get my husband to buff it out with this special car wax he ordered online. He spent like two hours watching YouTube tutorials on how to apply it... \n\nUm, but yeah, sorry. Making decisions and thinking clearly? No, I haven't noticed any changes with that. I'm doing okay there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don't get as much pleasure out of the things you usually enjoy, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure out of things? Um, no, not really. I mean, I still do all the stuff I usually do. Like, I still read before bed. I'm reading this mystery novel right now, well, it's more of a cozy mystery. It's set in this little bakery in Vermont, and the main character's a baker who solves murders on the side. It's kind of silly, but the recipes she describes sound amazing. She made these blueberry scones in chapter four that made my mouth water. I actually tried to bake scones once, years ago. It was for my sister's baby shower. I completely messed up the butter-to-flour ratio, and they came out like little hockey pucks. My brother-in-law joked that we could use them to level the wobbly table on their patio. That patio was so nice, though. They had this beautiful wrought-iron furniture set that they got at an estate sale. I love estate sales, you never know what you're going to find. I found this gorgeous vintage globe at one last summer. It's sitting on my desk in the classroom now. The kids love spinning it, even though it's so old that half the countries have different names now...\n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again. Um, but no, I haven't noticed losing pleasure in things. I still enjoy my books and everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the things you usually enjoy, like reading or being in your garden, don’t feel as satisfying or enjoyable as they used to, even though you still do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, I still like reading, and my garden is doing okay, too. I was actually out there yesterday checking on the tomatoes. I decided to try growing those purple heirloom ones this year. I got the seeds from this huge catalog my mother-in-law sent me. She lives down in Florida now, in this retirement community that has a golf cart parade every Fourth of July. They decorate the carts with streamers and everything, it's wild. Anyway, she's always sending me the most random stuff in the mail. Just last week, she sent a huge box of grapefruits. They were massive, like the size of softballs. My husband tried to juice them for breakfast, but our juicer is this old plastic thing we got at a thrift store years ago. It makes this horrible grinding noise when you press down on it. It sounds exactly like the electric pencil sharpener in my classroom. I really need to put in a maintenance request for that sharpener, it just eats the kids' pencils. Little Timmy was trying to sharpen his colored pencils for a geography map assignment and it just chewed his entire green pencil right down to the metal part... \n\nUm, but wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, enjoying things. No, I haven't noticed any changes with that. I still enjoy my garden and everything just fine.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like reading or working in your garden, do you find that they don’t give you the same sense of satisfaction or happiness they used to?\n\nPatient: I feel like we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, honestly, it's the same as it's always been. I don't feel any different when I'm doing my hobbies. I mean, my garden is actually doing better than last year. The marigolds I planted around the border are really popping. I read online that they keep the bugs away from the vegetables, which is great because last summer we had this awful problem with Japanese beetles. They just decimated the basil. I couldn't even make pesto. And I love making pesto from scratch. I use pine nuts, but they've gotten so expensive lately. I was at the grocery store on Tuesday, the one over on 4th Street, and a tiny little jar was like eight dollars. I almost dropped it. The aisles in that store are so narrow, too. You can barely fit two carts past each other. I always end up getting stuck behind someone who's just staring at the cereal boxes for ten minutes. It's like, just pick the Cheerios, you know? They have like fifty different kinds of Cheerios now anyway. Honey Nut, Apple Cinnamon, even Chocolate... I don't know who's letting their kids eat chocolate cereal for breakfast, but I see it in my classroom. The sugar crash hits right around 10 AM, right in the middle of math...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. Yeah, I'm still getting the same happiness out of things. Nothing's changed there.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like reading or being in your garden, do you find that they don’t feel as rewarding or make you as happy as they used to?\n\nPatient: I'm sorry, but are we going in circles? I feel like you keep asking me the exact same thing. No, really, everything still feels just as rewarding as it always did. I'm perfectly happy with my hobbies. \n\nActually, I was just looking at my garden this morning thinking I need to get some new gardening gloves. My old ones have a hole right in the thumb. I got them at that hardware store on Main Street, the one that used to have the really sweet golden retriever that would greet you at the door? They sold the place a few months ago and the new owners don't allow pets inside anymore, which is such a shame. The kids in my class were so upset when I told them. Half of them used to go in there with their parents on the weekends just to pet the dog. Kids get so attached to animals, you know? We have a class hamster named Barnaby, and honestly, cleaning his cage is the bane of my existence. The wood shavings get absolutely everywhere, and the custodian, Mr. Henderson, always gives me this look when he comes in to sweep at the end of the day. He's retiring next year, and I think he's just completely checked out at this point... \n\nUm, but anyway. No, I haven't lost any happiness from my reading or gardening. It's all completely fine. I promise!\n\nDoctor: Even though you still read and work in your garden, do you find that these activities don't bring you the same sense of joy or fulfillment they used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Okay, I feel like I'm taking a pop quiz where I keep giving the wrong answer. I really don't know how else to explain it, doctor. No. They still make me just as happy as they always have. \n\nLike, I'm reading this new historical fiction book right now, and I seriously can't put it down. It's for my book club that meets on Thursdays. We usually meet at Sarah's house—she's the media specialist at my school. She always bakes these incredible treats for us when it's her turn to host. Last week she made these little lemon tartlets that were just to die for. I asked her for the recipe, but she said she got it from her grandmother who used to run a bakery in Chicago. I've never been to Chicago, actually. My husband went there for a conference a few years ago and said the wind really is as bad as everyone says. He brought me back this snow globe from the Navy Pier, but one of the kids in my class accidentally knocked it off my desk last month. It completely shattered. There was water and fake snow everywhere, right in the middle of a spelling test. I had to use an entire roll of paper towels to sop it up before it ruined the grading rubric I had sitting there... \n\nAnyway. I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought again. But yes, to answer your question, I still get plenty of fulfillment from the things I do. I haven't noticed any drop in how much joy they bring me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Are you... are you messing with me? Is this a test? Because I feel like I'm losing my mind here. I swear you just asked me that. Like, word for word. 

But, okay, I'll say it again. No. I still enjoy reading and gardening exactly the same amount. Nothing has changed. 

I actually just started a new book yesterday, a mystery this time. I usually stick to historical fiction, but my sister recommended this one. He really struggled with his multiplication tables...

But, uh, what were we talking about? Right, my hobbies. Yes, they still make me happy. I'm really not having any issues with that.

I'm just so exhausted while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the day, even when you're not busy or tired?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, I guess I do. It's like... most days I can still put on a smile for my students, you know? Get through the lesson plans. We're actually doing a unit on the solar system right now, and I had them build these little dioramas. One kid, Tommy, he used a golf ball for Jupiter and painted it with nail polish. It smelled awful, but it was so creative! I had to open all the windows in the classroom, which made the principal walk by and give me this look... he always wears these really loud ties, by the way. Like, neon geometric patterns. It's very distracting during staff meetings. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the mood thing. Yeah, even when I'm smiling at the kids, underneath it all, there's just this... this gray, flat feeling. It just kind of follows me home. Like I'm just disconnected from it, even though I want to do it. It's just... always there, that flat feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough, or have you been blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not really your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I do. I just keep thinking I should be handling everything better, you know? Like, my lesson plans are taking twice as long to finish. And the principal actually asked me to mentor a newer teacher this semester, Miss Davis. She’s fresh out of college, super sweet, but she wears these incredibly high heels to work every single day. I honestly don't know how she does it on those hard school tile floors. My feet would be screaming by second period. I actually finally caved and bought these orthopedic inserts last month—total game changer, by the way. I got them at that pharmacy down on 4th street, the one with the really grumpy pharmacist who always looks like he's judging whatever you buy? I went in there to get the inserts and a pack of peppermint gum, and he just glared at me the whole time I was checking out... \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, blaming myself. Yeah, so I had to say no to mentoring Miss Davis because I just don't have the capacity for it right now. And when I do things like that, or when I'm just dragging through my work, I just tell myself I’m letting my family down, and my school down. It's just this constant feeling that I'm dropping the ball.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, I feel guilty and exhausted, but I don't want to hurt myself. I just want to feel better, you know? Like, I'd really love to just take a vacation somewhere and rest. My friend Sarah just got back from this trip to Sedona, and she was telling me all about these vortexes and how the energy there is supposed to be so healing. She brought back this little rose quartz crystal for me. I put it on my desk next to my pen cup—the one shaped like a little school bus that one of my students got me for Teacher Appreciation Week last year. It’s actually really cute, but the wheels keep falling off. I’ve tried supergluing them back on like three times, but I always manage to glue my fingers together instead. The last time I did it, I had to use nail polish remover to get my fingers unstuck, and it completely ruined my manicure. I usually just do a clear coat, but I had actually tried this really pretty pale pink color for my cousin's wedding... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, right, hurting myself. No, definitely not. I don't have any thoughts like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these feelings of exhaustion and low mood have been present nearly every day, and last throughout most of the day, even when you're not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess so? I'm not really sure if it's every single day, or if it lasts all day long. It's hard to keep track of time lately. Speaking of time, the clock in my classroom has been broken for like three weeks now. The second hand just twitches in place, right on the twelve. It makes this little *tick-tick-tick* sound that drives me absolutely up the wall when the kids are doing silent reading. I put in a maintenance request with Gary, our custodian, but he's been super busy because someone clogged the boys' bathroom on the second floor with paper towels. Gary's great, though. He always brings his golden retriever, Buster, to the school on weekends when he's doing deep cleaning. Buster is so fluffy, he looks like a giant toasted marshmallow. I tried to bring him some dog treats once, but they got squished in my bag under a stack of math worksheets... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, about the exhaustion being there every day. Yeah, I don't know if it's constantly there or not. I haven't really paid that much attention to exactly when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if most days you feel this low mood and lack of energy, even if some days are a little better than others?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... yeah, most days. Like, I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, you know? I have to. Especially with my third graders, they pick up on everything. I know exactly when it started because it was right around the time my husband stopped working outside the home. Our whole household routine just completely flipped upside down. He started doing this freelance graphic design stuff from the guest room, which is great, but he completely took over the space. He bought this massive ergonomic chair that looks like something out of a spaceship, and it barely fits through the door. And he drinks so much coffee now, he bought one of those fancy espresso machines that grinds the beans right there. It sounds like a jet engine taking off every morning at 7 AM. I used to like the smell of coffee, but now it just reminds me of how messy the kitchen counter gets because he never wipes up the coffee grounds. I tried buying him one of those little countertop vacuums—it looked like a ladybug, it was so cute—but he said it didn't have enough suction. The cafeteria ladies are usually pretty nice, though. Brenda, she's the one who always wears those holiday-themed aprons—she had one with little dancing turkeys on it last November—she always makes sure to give me an extra scoop of corn if they have it. She says I look like I need the energy. I actually bumped into Brenda at the farmer's market last weekend. She was buying these massive heirloom tomatoes. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels kept taking one bite out of them and leaving them on the fence. It's so frustrating! I bought this special netting to put over the plants, but it just got tangled in the rose bushes... \n\nWait, sorry, you were asking about my appetite? Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed eating any more or less than usual. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Okay, is your screen frozen or something? Are you reading off a broken script? Because I am a hundred percent positive you just asked me that exact same question. This is the fourth time! 

But... fine. I'll say it again. No. The answer is still no. I still get exactly the same amount of fulfillment and joy from my garden and my books. 

Honestly, this is starting to feel like when I have to remind my third graders to put their names on their spelling tests. I swear I say it fifty times a week. "Names on the top right, please!" And without fail, Kevin hands in a nameless paper. He’s a sweet kid, but his head is just completely in the clouds. His mom says he gets it from his dad, who plays the saxophone in this local jazz band. I actually went to see them play once at that little Italian bistro downtown, the one with the green awning? The music was great, but the food was just awful. I ordered the chicken piccata and it was so salty I couldn't even swallow it. The waitress felt so bad she gave me a free slice of cherry cheesecake. I usually hate cherry flavor because it reminds me of that terrible red cough syrup my mom used to make me take when I had bronchitis as a kid. It was so thick and sticky, and it actually stained my favorite pajama top... the one with the little dancing bears on it. I cried for two days over that shirt. 

I... wait, why am I talking about cough syrup? Oh, right. Look, doctor, I don't know how else to tell you. I enjoy my hobbies just fine. Can we please, please move on to a different question now?
